People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. I came in because… well, since my wife’s fall, I don’t recognize myself. I used to be the one with the lists, the plan, the backup plan — I kept things moving. And now I’m sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart like it’s written in another language, and I’ll just start crying, or I’ll snap over some tiny thing that doesn’t matter.

Bills are piling up, the housework is piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls I never would have missed before. It’s like I can see exactly what needs doing, but I can’t make myself function. I just… I’m not managing.

Yes… most of the time, I think so. It’s not always like I’m sitting there thinking, “I’m sad,” in a clear way, but since her fall there’s this heaviness over everything. I’ll be at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and I used to be able to handle things like that — lists, schedules, bills, all of it — and now I just stare at it and start crying.

And then other times it comes out as snapping over nothing, some tiny thing that shouldn’t matter. Then I feel awful, because I know I’m not acting like myself. The bills are piling up, the housework is piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls I never would have missed before. So yes, I’d say I’ve been sad a lot of the time, but it’s mixed in with this feeling that I just can’t function the way I used to.

Yes… anxious is part of it, I think. Since my wife’s fall, I feel like I’m constantly braced for the next thing I’m going to fail at. I used to be the one who had the list, the plan, the backup plan — that was sort of my role. I stare at it, and my mind starts spinning, and then I’m crying or I snap over something completely small.

It’s not just worry in my head, either. It’s like everything piles up at once — the bills, the housework, the mentoring calls I’ve missed — and I can see all of it, but I can’t get myself moving the way I used to. So yes, I’ve been anxious… and ashamed of how unable I feel to manage things.

I’ll be at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and I used to be able to handle things like that — lists, schedules, bills, all of it — and now I just stare at it and start crying.\n\nAnd then other times it comes out as snapping over nothing, some tiny thing that shouldn’t matter. Then I feel awful, because I know I’m not acting like myself. The bills are piling up, the housework is piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls I never would have missed before. So yes, I’d say I’ve been sad a lot of the time, but it’s mixed in with this feeling that I just can’t function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is part of it, I think. Since my wife’s fall, I feel like I’m constantly braced for the next thing I’m going to fail at. I used to be the one who had the list, the plan, the backup plan — that was sort of my role. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and instead of just working through it, I freeze. I stare at it, and my mind starts spinning, and then I’m crying or I snap over something completely small.\n\nIt’s not just worry in my head, either. It’s like everything piles up at once — the bills, the housework, the mentoring calls I’ve missed — and I can see all of it, but I can’t get myself moving the way I used to. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I suppose it might look that way from the outside, because I’m not really doing much for pleasure. But I can’t tell if I’ve lost interest exactly, or if I just don’t have the room in my head for anything beyond what’s in front of me.

There were things I used to like, ordinary things, but when I think about them now it’s not that I feel, “I never want to do that again.” It’s more like they don’t even come up on the list. I’m not sitting there longing to do them, but I’m also not sure I’ve consciously stopped caring. Everything just feels pushed to the side.

So… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it clearly as “losing interest.” I’m not sure. It’s been hard to separate what I want from what I’m just too overwhelmed to even think about.

I haven’t really been keeping track of enjoyment, if that makes sense. Most of the things I used to do are kind of blurred together right now, because everything seems to revolve around what needs to be handled for my wife or the house.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve sat down and thought, “Do I enjoy this or not?” It’s more like I don’t get around to doing much of anything in the same way I used to, so it’s hard for me to tell whether the enjoyment is gone or whether I’m just not in the right frame of mind to notice it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where everything feels like too much, and I’ve thought, “How am I supposed to keep doing this?” but that’s not the same as wanting to hurt myself, at least not how I understand it.

I haven’t made any plans or pictured doing something to myself. It’s more that I get worn down and scared and sort of stuck in my own head. But no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts of harming myself.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… well, since my wife’s fall, I don’t recognize myself. I used to be the one with the lists, the plan, the backup plan — I kept things moving. And now I’m sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart like it’s written in another language, and I’ll just start crying, or I’ll snap over some tiny thing that doesn’t matter.\n\nBills are piling up, the housework is piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls I never would have missed before. It’s like I can see exactly what needs doing, but I can’t make myself function. I just… I’m not managing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t really been keeping track of enjoyment, if that makes sense. Most of the things I used to do are kind of blurred together right now, because everything seems to revolve around what needs to be handled for my wife or the house.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve sat down and thought, “Do I enjoy this or not?” It’s more like I don’t get around to doing much of anything in the same way I used to, so it’s hard for me to tell whether the enjoyment is gone or whether I’m just not in the right frame of mind to notice it. I’m sorry, that’s probably not a very direct answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think so. It’s not always like I’m sitting there thinking, “I’m sad,” in a clear way, but since her fall there’s this heaviness over everything. I’ll be at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and I used to be able to handle things like that — lists, schedules, bills, all of it — and now I just stare at it and start crying.\n\nAnd then other times it comes out as snapping over nothing, some tiny thing that shouldn’t matter. Then I feel awful, because I know I’m not acting like myself. The bills are piling up, the housework is piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls I never would have missed before. So yes, I’d say I’ve been sad a lot of the time, but it’s mixed in with this feeling that I just can’t function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is part of it, I think. Since my wife’s fall, I feel like I’m constantly braced for the next thing I’m going to fail at. I used to be the one who had the list, the plan, the backup plan — that was sort of my role. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and instead of just working through it, I freeze. I stare at it, and my mind starts spinning, and then I’m crying or I snap over something completely small.\n\nIt’s not just worry in my head, either. It’s like everything piles up at once — the bills, the housework, the mentoring calls I’ve missed — and I can see all of it, but I can’t get myself moving the way I used to. So yes, I’ve been anxious… and ashamed of how unable I feel to manage things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've been able to enjoy or find pleasure in since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wish I could point to something and say, yes, I still enjoy that, but I haven’t been paying attention to pleasure in that way. Most of the time, if I do something, it’s because it has to be done, not because I’m choosing it for enjoyment.\n\nThere may be small moments where I’m distracted for a bit, or where something feels a little less heavy, but I don’t know if I’d call that enjoying it. I haven’t really had the mental space to notice, if that makes sense. It all feels sort of… muted. So I can’t honestly give you a clear example.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to find pleasurable since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I suppose it might look that way from the outside, because I’m not really doing much for pleasure. But I can’t tell if I’ve lost interest exactly, or if I just don’t have the room in my head for anything beyond what’s in front of me.\n\nThere were things I used to like, ordinary things, but when I think about them now it’s not that I feel, “I never want to do that again.” It’s more like they don’t even come up on the list. I’m not sitting there longing to do them, but I’m also not sure I’ve consciously stopped caring. Everything just feels pushed to the side.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it clearly as “losing interest.” I’m not sure. It’s been hard to separate what I want from what I’m just too overwhelmed to even think about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I suppose if it were significant I might have noticed, but I’ve been so focused on getting through each day that I may not be the best judge of that right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t have put it that way at first. It’s not like I’m deliberately saying, “I’m going to avoid people,” but I’ve stopped showing up for things I used to be part of.

I used to do some mentoring, and there were community commitments I kept up with, things where people counted on me to be there. And now I just… I don’t go. Sometimes I tell myself I’ll get back to them next week, or that I just need to get through whatever is right in front of me, but then another week goes by. I feel bad about it, because those were meaningful things, and I know I’m letting things drop.

And it’s not only outside commitments. Even ordinary things at home are slipping badly. Bills, little upkeep around the house, basic household routines that I used to handle almost automatically — they pile up now. I’ll see something that needs doing and just feel like I can’t quite make myself start, or I’ll put it aside and then it becomes this bigger, more embarrassing mess.

So yes, I think I have been pulling back from activities and people, even if I haven’t been thinking of it as avoidance exactly. It’s more like my world has gotten very small.

Then I feel awful, because I know I’m not acting like myself. The bills are piling up, the housework is piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls I never would have missed before. So yes, I’d say I’ve been sad a lot of the time, but it’s mixed in with this feeling that I just can’t function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is part of it, I think. Since my wife’s fall, I feel like I’m constantly braced for the next thing I’m going to fail at. I used to be the one who had the list, the plan, the backup plan — that was sort of my role. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and instead of just working through it, I freeze. I haven’t really had the mental space to notice, if that makes sense. It all feels sort of… muted. So I can’t honestly give you a clear example.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to find pleasurable since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I suppose it might look that way from the outside, because I’m not really doing much for pleasure. But I can’t tell if I’ve lost interest exactly, or if I just don’t have the room in my head for anything beyond what’s in front of me.\n\nThere were things I used to like, ordinary things, but when I think about them now it’s not that I feel, “I never want to do that again.” It’s more like they don’t even come up on the list. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… well, since my wife’s fall, I don’t recognize myself. I used to be the one with the lists, the plan, the backup plan — I kept things moving. And now I’m sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart like it’s written in another language, and I’ll just start crying, or I’ll snap over some tiny thing that doesn’t matter.\n\nBills are piling up, the housework is piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls I never would have missed before. It’s like I can see exactly what needs doing, but I can’t make myself function. I just… I’m not managing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t really been keeping track of enjoyment, if that makes sense. Most of the things I used to do are kind of blurred together right now, because everything seems to revolve around what needs to be handled for my wife or the house.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve sat down and thought, “Do I enjoy this or not?” It’s more like I don’t get around to doing much of anything in the same way I used to, so it’s hard for me to tell whether the enjoyment is gone or whether I’m just not in the right frame of mind to notice it. I’m sorry, that’s probably not a very direct answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think so. It’s not always like I’m sitting there thinking, “I’m sad,” in a clear way, but since her fall there’s this heaviness over everything. I’ll be at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and I used to be able to handle things like that — lists, schedules, bills, all of it — and now I just stare at it and start crying.\n\nAnd then other times it comes out as snapping over nothing, some tiny thing that shouldn’t matter. Then I feel awful, because I know I’m not acting like myself. The bills are piling up, the housework is piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls I never would have missed before. So yes, I’d say I’ve been sad a lot of the time, but it’s mixed in with this feeling that I just can’t function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is part of it, I think. Since my wife’s fall, I feel like I’m constantly braced for the next thing I’m going to fail at. I used to be the one who had the list, the plan, the backup plan — that was sort of my role. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and instead of just working through it, I freeze. I stare at it, and my mind starts spinning, and then I’m crying or I snap over something completely small.\n\nIt’s not just worry in my head, either. It’s like everything piles up at once — the bills, the housework, the mentoring calls I’ve missed — and I can see all of it, but I can’t get myself moving the way I used to. So yes, I’ve been anxious… and ashamed of how unable I feel to manage things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've been able to enjoy or find pleasure in since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wish I could point to something and say, yes, I still enjoy that, but I haven’t been paying attention to pleasure in that way. Most of the time, if I do something, it’s because it has to be done, not because I’m choosing it for enjoyment.\n\nThere may be small moments where I’m distracted for a bit, or where something feels a little less heavy, but I don’t know if I’d call that enjoying it. I haven’t really had the mental space to notice, if that makes sense. It all feels sort of… muted. So I can’t honestly give you a clear example.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to find pleasurable since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I suppose it might look that way from the outside, because I’m not really doing much for pleasure. But I can’t tell if I’ve lost interest exactly, or if I just don’t have the room in my head for anything beyond what’s in front of me.\n\nThere were things I used to like, ordinary things, but when I think about them now it’s not that I feel, “I never want to do that again.” It’s more like they don’t even come up on the list. I’m not sitting there longing to do them, but I’m also not sure I’ve consciously stopped caring. Everything just feels pushed to the side.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it clearly as “losing interest.” I’m not sure. It’s been hard to separate what I want from what I’m just too overwhelmed to even think about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself or tracking it in any organized way, and my clothes… I don’t know, I haven’t noticed anything dramatic enough that it’s made me stop and think, “Oh, I’ve really lost weight,” or “I’ve put weight on.”\n\nIt’s possible there’s been some change just because everything has been so unsettled, but I couldn’t give you a number, or even say clearly which direction. I suppose if it were significant I might have noticed, but I’ve been so focused on getting through each day that I may not be the best judge of that right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where everything feels like too much, and I’ve thought, “How am I supposed to keep doing this?” but that’s not the same as wanting to hurt myself, at least not how I understand it.\n\nI haven’t made any plans or pictured doing something to myself. It’s more that I get worn down and scared and sort of stuck in my own head. But no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts of harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t have put it that way at first. It’s not like I’m deliberately saying, “I’m going to avoid people,” but I’ve stopped showing up for things I used to be part of.\n\nI used to do some mentoring, and there were community commitments I kept up with, things where people counted on me to be there. And now I just… I don’t go. Sometimes I tell myself I’ll get back to them next week, or that I just need to get through whatever is right in front of me, but then another week goes by. I feel bad about it, because those were meaningful things, and I know I’m letting things drop.\n\nAnd it’s not only outside commitments. Even ordinary things at home are slipping badly. Bills, little upkeep around the house, basic household routines that I used to handle almost automatically — they pile up now. I’ll see something that needs doing and just feel like I can’t quite make myself start, or I’ll put it aside and then it becomes this bigger, more embarrassing mess.\n\nSo yes, I think I have been pulling back from activities and people, even if I haven’t been thinking of it as avoidance exactly. It’s more like my world has gotten very small.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to one clear thing and say, “That’s what I’m guilty about.” It’s more… a general feeling, I suppose. Like I’m not handling things the way I should be, or I should be doing more, or doing it better.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether there’s some specific act or decision I keep replaying in my mind, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to invent something just to have an answer. It’s more that guilt kind of floats around everything now, if that makes sense, but I can’t always say exactly what it attaches to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’m not sure I’d call it numb, exactly. I do have times where I feel sort of shut down, or like I’m moving through the day without really taking things in, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being emotionally numb most of the time.

I still feel things — sometimes too much, honestly — so “numb” doesn’t feel quite right. Maybe there are stretches where I’m blank or distant, but I haven’t noticed it as this constant lack of feeling. I think I’d need to understand what you mean by numb a little more, because I may be using the word differently than you are.

  {
    "role": "system",

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… well, since my wife’s fall, I don’t recognize myself. I used to be the one with the lists, the plan, the backup plan — I kept things moving. And now I’m sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart like it’s written in another language, and I’ll just start crying, or I’ll snap over some tiny thing that doesn’t matter.\n\nBills are piling up, the housework is piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls I never would have missed before. It’s like I can see exactly what needs doing, but I can’t make myself function. I just… I’m not managing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t really been keeping track of enjoyment, if that makes sense. Most of the things I used to do are kind of blurred together right now, because everything seems to revolve around what needs to be handled for my wife or the house.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve sat down and thought, “Do I enjoy this or not?” It’s more like I don’t get around to doing much of anything in the same way I used to, so it’s hard for me to tell whether the enjoyment is gone or whether I’m just not in the right frame of mind to notice it. I’m sorry, that’s probably not a very direct answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think so. It’s not always like I’m sitting there thinking, “I’m sad,” in a clear way, but since her fall there’s this heaviness over everything. I’ll be at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and I used to be able to handle things like that — lists, schedules, bills, all of it — and now I just stare at it and start crying.\n\nAnd then other times it comes out as snapping over nothing, some tiny thing that shouldn’t matter. Then I feel awful, because I know I’m not acting like myself. The bills are piling up, the housework is piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls I never would have missed before. So yes, I’d say I’ve been sad a lot of the time, but it’s mixed in with this feeling that I just can’t function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is part of it, I think. Since my wife’s fall, I feel like I’m constantly braced for the next thing I’m going to fail at. I used to be the one who had the list, the plan, the backup plan — that was sort of my role. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and instead of just working through it, I freeze. I stare at it, and my mind starts spinning, and then I’m crying or I snap over something completely small.\n\nIt’s not just worry in my head, either. It’s like everything piles up at once — the bills, the housework, the mentoring calls I’ve missed — and I can see all of it, but I can’t get myself moving the way I used to. So yes, I’ve been anxious… and ashamed of how unable I feel to manage things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've been able to enjoy or find pleasure in since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wish I could point to something and say, yes, I still enjoy that, but I haven’t been paying attention to pleasure in that way. Most of the time, if I do something, it’s because it has to be done, not because I’m choosing it for enjoyment.\n\nThere may be small moments where I’m distracted for a bit, or where something feels a little less heavy, but I don’t know if I’d call that enjoying it. I haven’t really had the mental space to notice, if that makes sense. It all feels sort of… muted. So I can’t honestly give you a clear example.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to find pleasurable since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I suppose it might look that way from the outside, because I’m not really doing much for pleasure. But I can’t tell if I’ve lost interest exactly, or if I just don’t have the room in my head for anything beyond what’s in front of me.\n\nThere were things I used to like, ordinary things, but when I think about them now it’s not that I feel, “I never want to do that again.” It’s more like they don’t even come up on the list. I’m not sitting there longing to do them, but I’m also not sure I’ve consciously stopped caring. Everything just feels pushed to the side.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it clearly as “losing interest.” I’m not sure. It’s been hard to separate what I want from what I’m just too overwhelmed to even think about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself or tracking it in any organized way, and my clothes… I don’t know, I haven’t noticed anything dramatic enough that it’s made me stop and think, “Oh, I’ve really lost weight,” or “I’ve put weight on.”\n\nIt’s possible there’s been some change just because everything has been so unsettled, but I couldn’t give you a number, or even say clearly which direction. I suppose if it were significant I might have noticed, but I’ve been so focused on getting through each day that I may not be the best judge of that right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where everything feels like too much, and I’ve thought, “How am I supposed to keep doing this?” but that’s not the same as wanting to hurt myself, at least not how I understand it.\n\nI haven’t made any plans or pictured doing something to myself. It’s more that I get worn down and scared and sort of stuck in my own head. But no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts of harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t have put it that way at first. It’s not like I’m deliberately saying, “I’m going to avoid people,” but I’ve stopped showing up for things I used to be part of.\n\nI used to do some mentoring, and there were community commitments I kept up with, things where people counted on me to be there. And now I just… I don’t go. Sometimes I tell myself I’ll get back to them next week, or that I just need to get through whatever is right in front of me, but then another week goes by. I feel bad about it, because those were meaningful things, and I know I’m letting things drop.\n\nAnd it’s not only outside commitments. Even ordinary things at home are slipping badly. Bills, little upkeep around the house, basic household routines that I used to handle almost automatically — they pile up now. I’ll see something that needs doing and just feel like I can’t quite make myself start, or I’ll put it aside and then it becomes this bigger, more embarrassing mess.\n\nSo yes, I think I have been pulling back from activities and people, even if I haven’t been thinking of it as avoidance exactly. It’s more like my world has gotten very small.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to one clear thing and say, “That’s what I’m guilty about.” It’s more… a general feeling, I suppose. Like I’m not handling things the way I should be, or I should be doing more, or doing it better.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether there’s some specific act or decision I keep replaying in my mind, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to invent something just to have an answer. It’s more that guilt kind of floats around everything now, if that makes sense, but I can’t always say exactly what it attaches to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're emotionally numb most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it numb, exactly. I do have times where I feel sort of shut down, or like I’m moving through the day without really taking things in, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being emotionally numb most of the time.\n\nI still feel things — sometimes too much, honestly — so “numb” doesn’t feel quite right. Maybe there are stretches where I’m blank or distant, but I haven’t noticed it as this constant lack of feeling. I think I’d need to understand what you mean by numb a little more, because I may be using the word differently than you are.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been about two months now, I think. Since the fall, really — that’s when everything seemed to change at home. Before that, there were ordinary stresses, of course, but this particular feeling, this pulling back and not quite managing things the way I used to… that’s been going on pretty much since then.

I keep thinking it’s only been a short time, and then I look back and realize weeks have gone by. So yes, about two months. It hasn’t felt like a single bad week or a few difficult days. It’s been kind of continuous since my wife’s fall.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… well, since my wife’s fall, I don’t recognize myself. I used to be the one with the lists, the plan, the backup plan — I kept things moving. And now I’m sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart like it’s written in another language, and I’ll just start crying, or I’ll snap over some tiny thing that doesn’t matter.\n\nBills are piling up, the housework is piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls I never would have missed before. It’s like I can see exactly what needs doing, but I can’t make myself function. I just… I’m not managing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t really been keeping track of enjoyment, if that makes sense. Most of the things I used to do are kind of blurred together right now, because everything seems to revolve around what needs to be handled for my wife or the house.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve sat down and thought, “Do I enjoy this or not?” It’s more like I don’t get around to doing much of anything in the same way I used to, so it’s hard for me to tell whether the enjoyment is gone or whether I’m just not in the right frame of mind to notice it. I’m sorry, that’s probably not a very direct answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think so. It’s not always like I’m sitting there thinking, “I’m sad,” in a clear way, but since her fall there’s this heaviness over everything. I’ll be at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and I used to be able to handle things like that — lists, schedules, bills, all of it — and now I just stare at it and start crying.\n\nAnd then other times it comes out as snapping over nothing, some tiny thing that shouldn’t matter. Then I feel awful, because I know I’m not acting like myself. The bills are piling up, the housework is piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls I never would have missed before. So yes, I’d say I’ve been sad a lot of the time, but it’s mixed in with this feeling that I just can’t function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is part of it, I think. Since my wife’s fall, I feel like I’m constantly braced for the next thing I’m going to fail at. I used to be the one who had the list, the plan, the backup plan — that was sort of my role. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and instead of just working through it, I freeze. I stare at it, and my mind starts spinning, and then I’m crying or I snap over something completely small.\n\nIt’s not just worry in my head, either. It’s like everything piles up at once — the bills, the housework, the mentoring calls I’ve missed — and I can see all of it, but I can’t get myself moving the way I used to. So yes, I’ve been anxious… and ashamed of how unable I feel to manage things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've been able to enjoy or find pleasure in since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wish I could point to something and say, yes, I still enjoy that, but I haven’t been paying attention to pleasure in that way. Most of the time, if I do something, it’s because it has to be done, not because I’m choosing it for enjoyment.\n\nThere may be small moments where I’m distracted for a bit, or where something feels a little less heavy, but I don’t know if I’d call that enjoying it. I haven’t really had the mental space to notice, if that makes sense. It all feels sort of… muted. So I can’t honestly give you a clear example.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to find pleasurable since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I suppose it might look that way from the outside, because I’m not really doing much for pleasure. But I can’t tell if I’ve lost interest exactly, or if I just don’t have the room in my head for anything beyond what’s in front of me.\n\nThere were things I used to like, ordinary things, but when I think about them now it’s not that I feel, “I never want to do that again.” It’s more like they don’t even come up on the list. I’m not sitting there longing to do them, but I’m also not sure I’ve consciously stopped caring. Everything just feels pushed to the side.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it clearly as “losing interest.” I’m not sure. It’s been hard to separate what I want from what I’m just too overwhelmed to even think about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself or tracking it in any organized way, and my clothes… I don’t know, I haven’t noticed anything dramatic enough that it’s made me stop and think, “Oh, I’ve really lost weight,” or “I’ve put weight on.”\n\nIt’s possible there’s been some change just because everything has been so unsettled, but I couldn’t give you a number, or even say clearly which direction. I suppose if it were significant I might have noticed, but I’ve been so focused on getting through each day that I may not be the best judge of that right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where everything feels like too much, and I’ve thought, “How am I supposed to keep doing this?” but that’s not the same as wanting to hurt myself, at least not how I understand it.\n\nI haven’t made any plans or pictured doing something to myself. It’s more that I get worn down and scared and sort of stuck in my own head. But no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts of harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t have put it that way at first. It’s not like I’m deliberately saying, “I’m going to avoid people,” but I’ve stopped showing up for things I used to be part of.\n\nI used to do some mentoring, and there were community commitments I kept up with, things where people counted on me to be there. And now I just… I don’t go. Sometimes I tell myself I’ll get back to them next week, or that I just need to get through whatever is right in front of me, but then another week goes by. I feel bad about it, because those were meaningful things, and I know I’m letting things drop.\n\nAnd it’s not only outside commitments. Even ordinary things at home are slipping badly. Bills, little upkeep around the house, basic household routines that I used to handle almost automatically — they pile up now. I’ll see something that needs doing and just feel like I can’t quite make myself start, or I’ll put it aside and then it becomes this bigger, more embarrassing mess.\n\nSo yes, I think I have been pulling back from activities and people, even if I haven’t been thinking of it as avoidance exactly. It’s more like my world has gotten very small.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to one clear thing and say, “That’s what I’m guilty about.” It’s more… a general feeling, I suppose. Like I’m not handling things the way I should be, or I should be doing more, or doing it better.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether there’s some specific act or decision I keep replaying in my mind, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to invent something just to have an answer. It’s more that guilt kind of floats around everything now, if that makes sense, but I can’t always say exactly what it attaches to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're emotionally numb most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it numb, exactly. I do have times where I feel sort of shut down, or like I’m moving through the day without really taking things in, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being emotionally numb most of the time.\n\nI still feel things — sometimes too much, honestly — so “numb” doesn’t feel quite right. Maybe there are stretches where I’m blank or distant, but I haven’t noticed it as this constant lack of feeling. I think I’d need to understand what you mean by numb a little more, because I may be using the word differently than you are.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months now, I think. Since the fall, really — that’s when everything seemed to change at home. Before that, there were ordinary stresses, of course, but this particular feeling, this pulling back and not quite managing things the way I used to… that’s been going on pretty much since then.\n\nI keep thinking it’s only been a short time, and then I look back and realize weeks have gone by.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Adjustment Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to one clear thing and say, “That’s what I’m guilty about.” It’s more… a general feeling, I suppose. Like I’m not handling things the way I should be, or I should be doing more, or doing it better.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether there’s some specific act or decision I keep replaying in my mind, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to invent something just to have an answer. It’s more that guilt kind of floats around everything now, if that makes sense, but I can’t always say exactly what it attaches to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're emotionally numb most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it numb, exactly. I do have times where I feel sort of shut down, or like I’m moving through the day without really taking things in, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being emotionally numb most of the time.\n\nI still feel things — sometimes too much, honestly — so “numb” doesn’t feel quite right. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I have. I suppose I’d say it’s less that I’m having arguments with people, and more that I’ve just… disappeared from things. I used to mentor a few people, and I had community commitments I was quite regular about — the sort of things where people expected me to show up, and I wanted to show up. Now I’ve stopped going, or I cancel, or I don’t respond properly when someone reaches out.

And at home, it’s affected things too. I know you asked about relationships, but it all blends together a bit. Things I used to manage without even thinking — bills, little repairs, keeping the house in order, basic routines — they’re slipping badly. And when those things slip, it creates distance. I don’t feel like I’m holding up my end of ordinary life the way I used to.

People have been kind, mostly, but I can tell I’m not as present. I’m not showing up for them, and I’m not showing up for the roles I used to have. That’s been one of the harder parts to admit.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… well, since my wife’s fall, I don’t recognize myself. So yes, I’d say I’ve been sad a lot of the time, but it’s mixed in with this feeling that I just can’t function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is part of it, I think. Since my wife’s fall, I feel like I’m constantly braced for the next thing I’m going to fail at. I used to be the one who had the list, the plan, the backup plan — that was sort of my role. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and instead of just working through it, I freeze. I stare at it, and my mind starts spinning, and then I’m crying or I snap over something completely small.\n\nIt’s not just worry in my head, either. It’s like everything piles up at once — the bills, the housework, the mentoring calls I’ve missed — and I can see all of it, but I can’t get myself moving the way I used to. So yes, I’ve been anxious… and ashamed of how unable I feel to manage things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've been able to enjoy or find pleasure in since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wish I could point to something and say, yes, I still enjoy that, but I haven’t been paying attention to pleasure in that way. Most of the time, if I do something, it’s because it has to be done, not because I’m choosing it for enjoyment.\n\nThere may be small moments where I’m distracted for a bit, or where something feels a little less heavy, but I don’t know if I’d call that enjoying it. I haven’t really had the mental space to notice, if that makes sense. It all feels sort of… muted. So I can’t honestly give you a clear example.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to find pleasurable since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I suppose it might look that way from the outside, because I’m not really doing much for pleasure. But I can’t tell if I’ve lost interest exactly, or if I just don’t have the room in my head for anything beyond what’s in front of me.\n\nThere were things I used to like, ordinary things, but when I think about them now it’s not that I feel, “I never want to do that again.” It’s more like they don’t even come up on the list. I’m not sitting there longing to do them, but I’m also not sure I’ve consciously stopped caring. Everything just feels pushed to the side.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it clearly as “losing interest.” I’m not sure. It’s been hard to separate what I want from what I’m just too overwhelmed to even think about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself or tracking it in any organized way, and my clothes… I don’t know, I haven’t noticed anything dramatic enough that it’s made me stop and think, “Oh, I’ve really lost weight,” or “I’ve put weight on.”\n\nIt’s possible there’s been some change just because everything has been so unsettled, but I couldn’t give you a number, or even say clearly which direction. I suppose if it were significant I might have noticed, but I’ve been so focused on getting through each day that I may not be the best judge of that right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. It’s more that I get worn down and scared and sort of stuck in my own head. not before it.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: Within 6 months of the termination of the stressor or its consequences\n  manifestation: There hasn’t really been an end to it yet—her mobility problems and the caregiving demands are still part of every day, so this has all been happening while the situation is ongoing.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve stopped showing up for mentoring and community commitments, and things I used to handle without Do you feel like you're emotionally numb most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it numb, exactly. I don’t feel like I’m holding up my end of ordinary life the way I used to.\n\nPeople have been kind, mostly, but I can tell I’m not as present. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I would say “consistently worthless,” no. Guilty… that’s harder to answer, because I do catch myself wondering whether I should be handling all of this better, or whether I’m letting people down in some way. But I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling guilty about myself all the time.

It’s more like… moments. I’ll think, “Why can’t I do this properly?” or “I ought to be stronger than this,” and then I get frustrated with myself. But I don’t walk around every minute thinking I’m worthless, if that’s what you mean. I still have some sense that I’m the same person underneath all this, even if I’m not functioning the way I’d like to.

So, no, not consistently. There are thoughts like that, but I wouldn’t describe it as a constant feeling that I’m worthless or bad.

It’s like I can see exactly what needs doing, but I can’t make myself function. I haven’t really been keeping track of enjoyment, if that makes sense. Most of the things I used to do are kind of blurred together right now, because everything seems to revolve around what needs to be handled for my wife or the house.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve sat down and thought, “Do I enjoy this or not?” It’s more like I don’t get around to doing much of anything in the same way I used to, so it’s hard for me to tell whether the enjoyment is gone or whether I’m just not in the right frame of mind to notice it. Since my wife’s fall, I feel like I’m constantly braced for the next thing I’m going to fail at. I used to be the one who had the list, the plan, the backup plan — that was sort of my role. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and instead of just working through it, I freeze. I stare at it, and my mind starts spinning, and then I’m crying or I snap over something completely small.\n\nIt’s not just worry in my head, either. It’s like everything piles up at once — the bills, the housework, the mentoring calls I’ve missed — and I can see all of it, but I can’t get myself moving the way I used to. So yes, I’ve been anxious… and ashamed of how unable I feel to manage things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've been able to enjoy or find pleasure in since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wish I could point to something and say, yes, I still enjoy that, but I haven’t been paying attention to pleasure in that way. Most of the time, if I do something, it’s because it has to be done, not because I’m choosing it for enjoyment.\n\nThere may be small moments where I’m distracted for a bit, or where something feels a little less heavy, but I don’t know if I’d call that enjoying it. I haven’t really had the mental space to notice, if that makes sense. It all feels sort of… muted. So I can’t honestly give you a clear example.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to find pleasurable since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I suppose it might look that way from the outside, because I’m not really doing much for pleasure. But I can’t tell if I’ve lost interest exactly, or if I just don’t have the room in my head for anything beyond what’s in front of me.\n\nThere were things I used to like, ordinary things, but when I think about them now it’s not that I feel, “I never want to do that again.” It’s more like they don’t even come up on the list. I’m not sitting there longing to do them, but I’m also not sure I’ve consciously stopped caring. Everything just feels pushed to the side.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it clearly as “losing interest.” I’m not sure. It’s been hard to separate what I want from what I’m just too overwhelmed to even think about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself or tracking it in any organized way, and my clothes… I don’t know, I haven’t noticed anything dramatic enough that it’s made me stop and think, “Oh, I’ve really lost weight,” or “I’ve put weight on.”\n\nIt’s possible there’s been some change just because everything has been so unsettled, but I couldn’t give you a number, or even say clearly which direction. I suppose if it were significant I might have noticed, but I’ve been so focused on getting through each day that I may not be the best judge of that right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where everything feels like too much, and I’ve thought, “How am I supposed to keep doing this?” but that’s not the same as wanting to hurt myself, at least not how I understand it.\n\nI haven’t made any plans or pictured doing something to myself. It’s more that I get worn down and scared and sort of stuck in my own head. But no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts of harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t have put it that way at first. It’s not like I’m deliberately saying, “I’m going to avoid people,” but I’ve stopped showing up for things I used to be part of.\n\nI used to do some mentoring, and there were community commitments I kept up with, things where people counted on me to be there. And now I just… I don’t go. Sometimes I tell myself I’ll get back to them next week, or that I just need to get through whatever is right in front of me, but then another week goes by. I feel bad about it, because those were meaningful things, and I know I’m letting things drop.\n\nAnd it’s not only outside commitments. Even ordinary things at home are slipping badly. Bills, little upkeep around the house, basic household routines that I used to handle almost automatically — they pile up now. I’ll see something that needs doing and just feel like I can’t quite make myself start, or I’ll put it aside and then it becomes this bigger, more embarrassing mess.\n\nSo yes, I think I have been pulling back from activities and people, even if I haven’t been thinking of it as avoidance exactly. I wouldn’t want to invent something just to have an answer. It’s more that I get worn down and scared and sort of stuck in my own head. But no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts of harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t have put it that way at first. It’s not like I’m deliberately saying, “I’m going to avoid people,” but I’ve stopped showing up for things I used to be part of.\n\nI used to do some mentoring, and there were community commitments I kept up with, things where people counted on me to be there. And now I just… I don’t go. Sometimes I tell myself I’ll get back to them next week, or that I just need to get through whatever is right in front of me, but then another week goes by. I feel bad about it, because those were meaningful things, and I know I’m letting things drop.\n\nAnd it’s not only outside commitments. Even ordinary things at home are slipping badly. Bills, little upkeep around the house, basic household routines that I used to handle almost automatically — they pile up now. I’ll see something that needs doing and just feel like I can’t quite make myself start, or I’ll put it aside and then it becomes this bigger, more embarrassing mess.\n\nSo yes, I think I have been pulling back from activities and people, even if I haven’t been thinking of it as avoidance exactly. It’s more like my world has gotten very small.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to one clear thing and say, “That’s what I’m guilty about.” It’s more… a general feeling, I suppose. Like I’m not handling things the way I should be, or I should be doing more, or doing it better.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether there’s some specific act or decision I keep replaying in my mind, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to invent something just to have an answer. It’s more that guilt kind of floats around everything now, if that makes sense, but I can’t always say exactly what it attaches to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're emotionally numb most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it numb, exactly. I do have times where I feel sort of shut down, or like I’m moving through the day without really taking things in, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being emotionally numb most of the time.\n\nI still feel things — sometimes too much, honestly — so “numb” doesn’t feel quite right. Maybe there are stretches where I’m blank or distant, but I haven’t noticed it as this constant lack of feeling. I think I’d need to understand what you mean by numb a little more, because I may be using the word differently than you are.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months now, I think. Since the fall, really — that’s when everything seemed to change at home. Before that, there were ordinary stresses, of course, but this particular feeling, this pulling back and not quite managing things the way I used to… that’s been going on pretty much since then.\n\nI keep thinking it’s only been a short time, and then I look back and realize weeks have gone by. So yes, about two months. It hasn’t felt like a single bad week or a few difficult days. It’s been kind of continuous since my wife’s fall.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulties in your relationships with others since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I suppose I’d say it’s less that I’m having arguments with people, and more that I’ve just… disappeared from things. I used to mentor a few people, and I had community commitments I was quite regular about — the sort of things where people expected me to show up, and I wanted to show up. Now I’ve stopped going, or I cancel, or I don’t respond properly when someone reaches out.\n\nAnd at home, it’s affected things too. I know you asked about relationships, but it all blends together a bit. Things I used to manage without even thinking — bills, little repairs, keeping the house in order, basic routines — they’re slipping badly. And when those things slip, it creates distance. I don’t feel like I’m holding up my end of ordinary life the way I used to.\n\nPeople have been kind, mostly, but I can tell I’m not as present. I’m not showing up for them, and I’m not showing up for the roles I used to have. That’s been one of the harder parts to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling consistently worthless or guilty about yourself since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I would say “consistently worthless,” no. Guilty… that’s harder to answer, because I do catch myself wondering whether I should be handling all of this better, or whether I’m letting people down in some way. But I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling guilty about myself all the time.\n\nIt’s more like… moments. I’ll think, “Why can’t I do this properly?” or “I ought to be stronger than this,” and then I get frustrated with myself. But I don’t walk around every minute thinking I’m worthless, if that’s what you mean. I still have some sense that I’m the same person underneath all this, even if I’m not functioning the way I’d like to.\n\nSo, no, not consistently. There are thoughts like that, but I wouldn’t describe it as a constant feeling that I’m worthless or bad.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… some things I used to enjoy, I’m not doing them as much, but I don’t know whether that’s because I’ve lost interest in them, or because everything feels harder to organize around right now.

I’ll be at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and I used to be able to handle things like that — lists, schedules, bills, all of it — and now I just stare at it and start crying.\n\nAnd then other times it comes out as snapping over nothing, some tiny thing that shouldn’t matter. Then I feel awful, because I know I’m not acting like myself. The bills are piling up, the housework is piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls I never would have missed before. So yes, I’d say I’ve been sad a lot of the time, but it’s mixed in with this feeling that I just can’t function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is part of it, I think. Since my wife’s fall, I feel like I’m constantly braced for the next thing I’m going to fail at. I used to be the one who had the list, the plan, the backup plan — that was sort of my role. Now I’ll sit at the kitchen table with her medication chart in front of me, and instead of just working through it, I freeze. I stare at it, and my mind starts spinning, and then I’m crying or I snap over something completely small.\n\nIt’s not just worry in my head, either. It’s like everything piles up at once — the bills, the housework, the mentoring calls I’ve missed — and I can see all of it, but I can’t get myself moving the way I used to. So yes, I’ve been anxious… and ashamed of how unable I feel to manage things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've been able to enjoy or find pleasure in since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wish I could point to something and say, yes, I still enjoy that, but I haven’t been paying attention to pleasure in that way. Most of the time, if I do something, it’s because it has to be done, not because I’m choosing it for enjoyment.\n\nThere may be small moments where I’m distracted for a bit, or where something feels a little less heavy, but I don’t know if I’d call that enjoying it. I haven’t really had the mental space to notice, if that makes sense. It all feels sort of… muted. So I can’t honestly give you a clear example.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to find pleasurable since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I suppose it might look that way from the outside, because I’m not really doing much for pleasure. But I can’t tell if I’ve lost interest exactly, or if I just don’t have the room in my head for anything beyond what’s in front of me.\n\nThere were things I used to like, ordinary things, but when I think about them now it’s not that I feel, “I never want to do that again.” It’s more like they don’t even come up on the list. I’m not sitting there longing to do them, but I’m also not sure I’ve consciously stopped caring. Everything just feels pushed to the side.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed it clearly as “losing interest.” I’m not sure. It’s been hard to separate what I want from what I’m just too overwhelmed to even think about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself or tracking it in any organized way, and my clothes… I don’t know, I haven’t noticed anything dramatic enough that it’s made me stop and think, “Oh, I’ve really lost weight,” or “I’ve put weight on.”\n\nIt’s possible there’s been some change just because everything has been so unsettled, but I couldn’t give you a number, or even say clearly which direction. I suppose if it were significant I might have noticed, but I’ve been so focused on getting through each day that I may not be the best judge of that right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where everything feels like too much, and I’ve thought, “How am I supposed to keep doing this?” but that’s not the same as wanting to hurt myself, at least not how I understand it.\n\nI haven’t made any plans or pictured doing something to myself. It’s more that I get worn down and scared and sort of stuck in my own head. But no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts of harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t have put it that way at first. It’s not like I’m deliberately saying, “I’m going to avoid people,” but I’ve stopped showing up for things I used to be part of.\n\nI used to do some mentoring, and there were community commitments I kept up with, things where people counted on me to be there. And now I just… I don’t go. Sometimes I tell myself I’ll get back to them next week, or that I just need to get through whatever is right in front of me, but then another week goes by. I feel bad about it, because those were meaningful things, and I know I’m letting things drop.\n\nAnd it’s not only outside commitments. Even ordinary things at home are slipping badly. Bills, little upkeep around the house, basic household routines that I used to handle almost automatically — they pile up now. I’ll see something that needs doing and just feel like I can’t quite make myself start, or I’ll put it aside and then it becomes this bigger, more embarrassing mess.\n\nSo yes, I think I have been pulling back from activities and people, even if I haven’t been thinking of it as avoidance exactly. It’s more like my world has gotten very small.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to one clear thing and say, “That’s what I’m guilty about.” It’s more… a general feeling, I suppose. Like I’m not handling things the way I should be, or I should be doing more, or doing it better.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether there’s some specific act or decision I keep replaying in my mind, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to invent something just to have an answer. It’s more that guilt kind of floats around everything now, if that makes sense, but I can’t always say exactly what it attaches to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're emotionally numb most of the time since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it numb, exactly. I do have times where I feel sort of shut down, or like I’m moving through the day without really taking things in, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being emotionally numb most of the time.\n\nI still feel things — sometimes too much, honestly — so “numb” doesn’t feel quite right. Maybe there are stretches where I’m blank or distant, but I haven’t noticed it as this constant lack of feeling. I think I’d need to understand what you mean by numb a little more, because I may be using the word differently than you are.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months now, I think. Since the fall, really — that’s when everything seemed to change at home. Before that, there were ordinary stresses, of course, but this particular feeling, this pulling back and not quite managing things the way I used to… that’s been going on pretty much since then.\n\nI keep thinking it’s only been a short time, and then I look back and realize weeks have gone by. So yes, about two months. It hasn’t felt like a single bad week or a few difficult days. It’s been kind of continuous since my wife’s fall.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulties in your relationships with others since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I suppose I’d say it’s less that I’m having arguments with people, and more that I’ve just… disappeared from things. I used to mentor a few people, and I had community commitments I was quite regular about — the sort of things where people expected me to show up, and I wanted to show up. Now I’ve stopped going, or I cancel, or I don’t respond properly when someone reaches out.\n\nAnd at home, it’s affected things too. I know you asked about relationships, but it all blends together a bit. Things I used to manage without even thinking — bills, little repairs, keeping the house in order, basic routines — they’re slipping badly. And when those things slip, it creates distance. I don’t feel like I’m holding up my end of ordinary life the way I used to.\n\nPeople have been kind, mostly, but I can tell I’m not as present. I’m not showing up for them, and I’m not showing up for the roles I used to have. That’s been one of the harder parts to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling consistently worthless or guilty about yourself since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I would say “consistently worthless,” no. Guilty… that’s harder to answer, because I do catch myself wondering whether I should be handling all of this better, or whether I’m letting people down in some way. But I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling guilty about myself all the time.\n\nIt’s more like… moments. I’ll think, “Why can’t I do this properly?” or “I ought to be stronger than this,” and then I get frustrated with myself. But I don’t walk around every minute thinking I’m worthless, if that’s what you mean. I still have some sense that I’m the same person underneath all this, even if I’m not functioning the way I’d like to.\n\nSo, no, not consistently. There are thoughts like that, but I wouldn’t describe it as a constant feeling that I’m worthless or bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, and I don’t think I’ve had some obvious, dramatic loss where my clothes are hanging off me or people are commenting on it.\n\nI mean, I may have gone up or down a little — at my age that happens, and with everything at home being unsettled I’m probably less attentive to those things than I used to be. But a significant amount? I don’t think I can say that. I haven’t noticed anything clear enough to call it that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in the things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… some things I used to enjoy, I’m not doing them as much, but I don’t know whether that’s because I’ve lost interest in them, or because everything feels harder to organize around right now.\n\nThere are activities and people and routines that used to give my week a shape, and I suppose I’ve stepped back from a lot of that. But if you asked me whether I still care about those things, I think I do. I don’t feel completely indifferent to them. It’s more that I don’t seem to get to the point of doing them, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly lost interest, no. Not in a simple way. It’s more muddled than that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Not really, no. I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting especially loose or anything like that. I mean, I’m not studying myself in the mirror every morning, and I tend to wear the same sort of comfortable things around the house, so maybe I wouldn’t be the first to pick up on a small change.
